id: "05.01"
course: "ai-research-track-web"
module: "05"
slug: "single-neuron-from-scratch"
title: "Single Neuron From Scratch"
question: "A single sigmoid neuron trained with squared error learns very slowly when its output starts near 0 or 1, even if badly wrong. Why, and which loss fixes it?"
answer: "The gradient contains <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1.0019em;vertical-align:-0.25em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\"><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0359em;\">σ</span><span class=\"msupsub\"><span class=\"vlist-t\"><span class=\"vlist-r\"><span class=\"vlist\" style=\"height:0.7519em;\"><span style=\"top:-3.063em;margin-right:0.05em;\"><span class=\"pstrut\" style=\"height:2.7em;\"></span><span class=\"katex-sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight\"><span class=\"mord mtight\"><span class=\"mord mtight\">′</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span><span class=\"mopen\">(</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.044em;\">z</span><span class=\"mclose\">)</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span><span class=\"mrel\">=</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">a</span><span class=\"mopen\">(</span><span class=\"mord\">1</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2222em;\"></span><span class=\"mbin\">−</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2222em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">a</span><span class=\"mclose\">)</span></span></span></span>, near 0 when the output saturates. Cross-entropy with a sigmoid cancels that factor: <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\" style=\"margin-right:0.0556em;\">∂</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">L</span><span class=\"mord\">/</span><span class=\"mord\" style=\"margin-right:0.0556em;\">∂</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.044em;\">z</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span><span class=\"mrel\">=</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6667em;vertical-align:-0.0833em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">a</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2222em;\"></span><span class=\"mbin\">−</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2222em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.625em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0359em;\">y</span></span></span></span>, so a confidently wrong neuron gets a large gradient."
sources: [{"title": "Michael Nielsen: Neural Networks and Deep Learning, chapter 1", "url": "http://neuralnetworksanddeeplearning.com/chap1.html", "note": "The gentlest introduction to neurons and weights."}, {"title": "3Blue1Brown: But what is a neural network?", "url": "https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=aircAruvnKk", "note": "A beautiful 19-minute picture of the whole idea."}, {"title": "Karpathy: micrograd", "url": "https://github.com/karpathy/micrograd", "note": "A neuron and backprop in about 100 lines of Python."}]
html: "<section class=\"art\"><h2>A single neuron, from scratch</h2><p class=\"cite\"><span class=\"lab\">Based on</span> Rosenblatt, &quot;The Perceptron&quot; (1958) · Goodfellow et al., Deep Learning §6.1–6.2 · Nielsen, Neural Networks and Deep Learning ch. 1 · Fanout 05.01</p><h3>Why this matters</h3>\n<p>Every neural network, including GPT-4-sized ones, is built from one repeated unit: a neuron that takes a weighted sum and squashes it. If you understand one neuron completely, including how it learns, the rest of this module is &quot;do that many times&quot;.</p>\n<h3>The idea in plain words</h3>\n<p>A neuron does three things:</p>\n<ol>\n<li><strong>Multiply</strong> each input by a weight. The weight says how much that input matters, and whether it pushes up or down.</li>\n<li><strong>Add</strong> them up, plus a bias (a constant nudge): <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.4306em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.044em;\">z</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span><span class=\"mrel\">=</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0359em;\">σ</span><span class=\"mopen\">(</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.044em;\">z</span><span class=\"mclose\">)</span></span></span></span>.</li>\n</ol>\n<p>Think of deciding whether to go for a run. Inputs: is it sunny (1 or 0), how tired are you (0 to 10). Weights: sun matters a lot (+2), tiredness counts against (−0.5). Bias: you generally like running (+1). Output: the probability you go.</p>\n<h3>Worked example 1: forward pass</h3>\n<p>Inputs <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span the four points: the 1s sit on one diagonal and the 0s on the other. No straight line separates them, so no single neuron can learn XOR. This limit (Minsky and Papert, 1969) is why we stack neurons into layers.</p>\n<h3>In code</h3>\n<pre class=\"code\"><code class=\"language-python\">import numpy as np\nx, w, b = np.array([2., 3.]), np.array([0.5, -1.]), 1.0\nz = w @ x + b                      # -1.0\np = 1 / (1 + np.exp(-z))           # 0.269\n\ny = 1.0\ndz = p - y                         # dL/dz for sigmoid + cross-entropy\nw -= 0.1 * dz * x                  # [0.646, -0.781]\nb -= 0.1 * dz                      # 1.073\n</code></pre>\n<h3>Common mistakes</h3>\n<ul>\n<li><strong>Forgetting the bias.</strong> Without it, every boundary line must pass through the origin.</li>\n<li><strong>Using MSE with a sigmoid output.</strong> It works, but the gradient becomes tiny when the neuron is confidently wrong. Cross-entropy gives <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.7778em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">p</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2222em;\"></span><span class=\"mbin\">−</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2222em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.625em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0359em;\">y</span></span></span></span>, which stays large exactly when you are wrong.</li>\n<li><strong>All weights starting at the same value.</strong> Neurons then stay identical forever. Start with small random weights.</li>\n<li><strong>Not scaling the inputs.</strong> If one input is in the thousands, its weight&#39;s updates dominate.</li>\n</ul>\n<h3>Try it section)</summary><section class=\"art\"><h2>From one neuron to a network</h2><p class=\"cite\"><span class=\"lab\">Based on</span> Goodfellow et al. §6.1-6.4 · Bishop and Bishop ch. 6, ch. 8 · d2l.ai §5.1-5.3 · Michael Nielsen, Neural Networks and Deep Learning ch. 1-2, 4 · Fanout 05.01-05.03</p><p>Nielsen builds networks up from a single neuron, and so does this lesson&#39;s sheet.</p>\n<p>A <strong>neuron</strong> computes a weighted sum of its inputs plus a bias, then applies a non-linearity: <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.4306em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">a</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span><span class=\"mrel\">=</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1.0991em;vertical-align:-0.25em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">ϕ</span><span class=\"mopen\">(</span><span class=\"mord\"><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0269em;\">w</span><span class=\"msupsub\"><span class=\"vlist-t\"><span class=\"vlist-r\"><span class=\"vlist\" style=\"height:0.8491em;\"><span mtight\">⊤</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">x</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2222em;\"></span><span class=\"mbin\">+</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2222em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">b</span><span class=\"mclose\">)</span></span></span></span>. A <strong>layer</strong> is many neurons sharing the same input, which is one matrix multiply: <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.4306em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">a</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span><span class=\"mrel\">=</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">ϕ</span><span class=\"mopen\">(</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.1389em;\">W</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">x</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2222em;\"></span><span class=\"mbin\">+</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2222em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">b</span><span class=\"mclose\">)</span></span></span></span>. fill: var(--ink)\" x=\"330.696\" y=\"84.671811\" transform=\"rotate(-0 330.696 84.671811)\">a = φ(w·x + b)</text> </g> <g id=\"text_10\"> <text style=\"font-size: 8.5px; font-family: 'Inter'; text-anchor: middle; fill: var(--muted)\" x=\"236.448\" y=\"148.064041\" transform=\"rotate(-0 236.448 148.064041)\">A layer is many of these sharing the same inputs: a = φ(Wx + b), one row of W per neuron.</text> </g> </g> </g> <defs> <clipPath id=\"p6a35d38d30\"> <rect x=\"3.6\" y=\"3.6\" width=\"465.696\" height=\"155.232\"/> </clipPath> </defs> </svg> </div><figcaption>One neuron: a weighted sum of inputs plus a bias, passed through a non-linearity <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.8889em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">ϕ</span></span></span></span>. A layer computes many of these at once from the same inputs, which is one matrix multiply.</figcaption></figure>\n<p>The last layer has no non-linearity; its outputs are logits that go into softmax and cross-entropy.</p>\n<h3>The XOR example</h3>\n<p>Goodfellow et al. §6.1 begin their chapter on feedforward networks with the smallest problem a linear model cannot solve: XOR, with inputs <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;\"></span><span class=\"mopen\">(</span><span class=\"mord\">0</span><span class=\"mpunct\">,</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.1667em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">0</span><span class=\"mclose\">)</span><span class=\"mpunct\">,</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.1667em;\"></span><span class=\"mopen\">(</span><span class=\"mord\">0</span><span class=\"mord\">0</span></span></span></span>. The hidden layer has moved the points into a space where a linear model works. That is the whole idea of representation learning in one example: <strong>the hidden layers learn features, and the last layer is a linear model on those features.</strong></p>\n<h3>Hidden units and output units</h3>\n<p>Goodfellow et al. §6.2-6.3 separate the two roles:</p>\n<ul>\n<li><strong>Output units</strong> are chosen by the likelihood (Lesson 01.11): linear outputs for a Gaussian (MSE), sigmoid for a Bernoulli (binary cross-entropy), softmax for a categorical. The pairing matters because the log in the loss undoes the exponential in the output, so the gradient does not vanish when the model is confidently wrong. Using a sigmoid output with MSE loss breaks this and saturates.</li>\n<li><strong>Hidden units</strong> are a design choice. ReLU is the default because it does not saturate for positive inputs and its gradient is exactly 1 there. Its weakness is &quot;dead&quot; units whose input is always negative and so never receive gradient. Leaky ReLU, GELU and SiLU/Swish keep a small gradient for negative inputs; GELU and SiLU are what transformers use (Lesson 01.02 plotted them). Sigmoid and tanh saturate at both ends, which is why they disappeared from hidden layers except inside gates (LSTMs, GLU variants).</li>\n</ul>\n<h3>Universal approximation and depth</h3>\n<p>The universal approximation theorem (Goodfellow et al. §6.4.1) says a network with one hidden layer and enough units can approximate any continuous function on a closed, bounded region to any accuracy. It says nothing about <strong>how many</strong> units are needed, or whether gradient descent will <strong>find</strong> them. Nielsen ch. 4 shows the construction visually: pairs of steep sigmoids make &quot;bumps&quot;, and enough bumps tile any function.</p>\n<p>The case for depth is efficiency. Goodfellow et al. discuss results (Montufar et al.) showing that a deep ReLU network can carve the input into a number of linear regions that grows <strong>exponentially</strong> with depth, while a shallow network with the same number of units grows only polynomially. Depth also matches a prior that is often right: that the function is a composition of simpler functions (edges, then textures, then parts, then objects).</p>\n<figure class=\"dg fig\"><div <clipPath id=\"pedae80c115\"> <rect x=\"11.873672\" y=\"19.771875\" width=\"221.76\" height=\"221.76\"/> </clipPath> </defs> </svg> </div><figcaption>A 2-100-3 MLP trained with full-batch gradient descent on three interleaved spirals, written in about 15 lines of NumPy using the four equations below. The coloured regions are the predicted class. No linear model gets above about a third of these points right.</figcaption></figure>\n<p>Why the non-linearity? Without it, the composition of linear maps is a single linear map, and depth buys nothing. With it, a network with one hidden layer can approximate any continuous function on a bounded region (the universal approximation theorem, which Nielsen&#39;s chapter 4 shows visually). Depth makes that approximation far more efficient for the kinds of functions real data needs.</p>\n<h3>Backprop for an MLP, in matrix form</h3>\n<p>Nielsen&#39;s chapter 2 states backprop as four equations. If a shape does not match, the order of a product or a transpose is wrong, and this check finds it faster than a debugger.</p>\n<p>The cost of backprop is about twice the cost of the forward pass: backward.</p>\n<p>Bishop and Bishop ch. 8 make the memory side explicit: backprop must keep every layer&#39;s input <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span style=\"height:0.15em;\"><span></span></span></span></span></span></span><span class=\"mclose\">)</span></span></span></span> until the backward pass reaches that layer. Memory for activations therefore grows linearly with depth and batch size, and is often larger than memory for the weights.</p>\n</section></details>"
lead: "Every neural network, including GPT-4-sized ones, is built from one repeated unit: a neuron that takes a weighted sum and squashes it. If you understand one neuron completely, including how it learns, the rest of this module is &quot;do that many times&quot;."
mins: 4
